<margin>foolish</margin>
10. The foolish fire-life had become dark through the impression of death. Therefore, it needed a light *entity* from the Divine Light to serve as a substance of light—like a holy anointing oil. In this oil, the dark, painful fire of the soul changes itself into a desire for light and love. This was conceived upon Christ’s *altar*—that is, out of Christ’s humanity, the substantial love and the substantial wisdom of God. That same fire within this substance was the *Name* and the power of JESUS: God’s greatest holiness, which changes the soul’s fire into a light. This is why Christ said, *“He was the light of the world”* (John 8:12). For by his power, the soul has again attained eyes of Divine Light, so that it beholds God once more.

11. Just as a person gives oil to a common fire to produce light, we should understand the dark fire of the soul in the same way. In this Testament <note>referring to the New Covenant/Sacrament</note>, the soul receives into itself Christ’s heavenly *entity* of Divine Substantial Love. This rekindles the vanished heavenly *entity* that was in *Adam* and burns in its likeness. In this process, the Kingdom of Heaven—the *City of God*—is unlocked again. The Divine Light shines once more in that unlocked paradisiacal ground, which is the City of God within *man* where the Holy Spirit dwells. Of this, Christ says: *“He that eats my flesh and drinks my blood continues in me and I in him”* (John 6:56).

12. In this unlocked paradisiacal ground, Christ—the Divine Love-*entity*—continues dwelling in the light. The power within the light is the Divine *entity*: the supernatural heavenly flesh, a tincture <note>term: "tincture"; in alchemy and mysticism, a spiritual essence or medicine that can transform and "color" a substance</note> of life. This tincture permeates the soul and gives a heavenly fire-glance to the soul’s fire. To understand this rightly: the soul is the Father’s fire, which receives into itself (into its fiery desire) the Son’s *substantial* love. This is the holy love-fire which has turned itself into the human tincture—into flesh and blood—meaning into the power of human life. In this way, the transformation of the soul’s will is achieved.

13. Thus, we first see this symbolic figure in *Moses’s* sacrifice of reconciliation: the soul (the faith-desire of the soul) brought itself into the sacrifice as if into a *substance*. With that substance, it entered the fire, where reconciliation was made through fire within the introduced Covenant of the love-fire. This happened in the same way that the substantial love of Christ would give itself up into the soul’s fire (where God’s anger burned) to serve as food and reconciliation. Secondly, we see through *Moses* not only how the soul is ransomed from sin through fire and fed with the holy fire of grace through the love in the Covenant, but also how they blessed the bread and flesh through the reconciliation of the Grace-Covenant,

and did eat the same, namely: the holy bread and flesh, as God also commanded them.

14. This signifies the oral eating and drinking of the Substantial Grace; where not only the soul is fed, but (Note) also the true Adamical man <note>original: "Adamicall Man"; referring to the original, uncorrupted human nature created in Adam</note>, *who shall rise from death again*. For the earth was cursed through man’s sin, and man was forced by that curse of God’s anger to eat of the earthly fruit. But with this sacrifice and blessing of the Covenant, God now establishes *a new blessing*, so that *Israel* ate of blessed food again, where Grace pressed through the curse once more, just as the sun presses and penetrates through water.

15. All of this was a *type* <note>original: "Type"; a biblical person, event, or ritual that foreshadows a future reality in Christ</note> of the Testament of Christ, which would not only feed the soul with substantial, fiery love, but (Note) also the true Adamical body. This was signified when he ordained his New Testament under the forms of bread and wine: it showed how the inward grace of love would press through the outward man, just as the sun penetrates through water, or as fire penetrates through iron.

16. The oral eating and drinking of his New Testament is signified by the Passover Lamb <note>original: "Paschal Lamb"</note> in the Old Testament. In that rite, the Grace-Covenant of the divine blessing gave itself into a visible, physical substance—namely, into a *medium* through which the human body received grace. This points toward the New Testament, where Christ—the Substantial Grace—would, with his love and the power of his flesh and blood (possessing both divine and human properties), *give himself* to his fellow-members, the Christians. He does this through a physical *medium* of blessed bread and wine for food and drink. Through this, the accursed body made from the *slime* <note>original: "Limus"; the dust or clay of the earth from which Adam was formed</note> of the earth (which has swallowed the curse into itself) is blessed once again.

17. We see this clearly in the Last Supper of Christ. With the institution of the New Testament, Christ did not intend to ordain any *strange* or *new thing*, but only to fulfill the Old Testament. With the Substantial Grace, which had become MAN in his soul and flesh, he gave himself up into the Covenant of the Old Testament. He himself became the *fulfillment*—namely, the Passover Lamb and the holy bread and flesh through which our true Adamical man is to be blessed. *For he first ate the Passover Lamb with his disciples and incorporated himself with them in the Covenant of the Old Testament, and washed their feet for them. Afterward, he took the bread, gave thanks, broke it, and gave it to them, saying: "Take and eat; this is my body, which is given for you." In the same way, he also took the cup after the supper, gave thanks, gave it to them, and said: "Drink all of you from it; this is my blood of the New Testament, which is shed for many for the forgiveness of sins. I say to you, I will from now on drink no more of this..."*

*...fruit of the vine, until the day that I will drink it new with you in my Father's Kingdom. Matthew 26, Mark 14, 1 Corinthians 11:23.*

18. This is now the true foundation, and it signifies, first, how the sweet grace of God's Covenant has given itself up into our humanity. Secondly, it shows how that same Grace-Covenant (which in the Old Testament gave itself to Israel as a blessing through sacrifices and the Paschal Lamb <note>The Passover lamb</note>) now gives itself to man through the assumed humanity <note>The human nature taken on by Christ</note> under a *medium*—namely, under bread and wine—to be eaten and drunk. Thirdly, it shows how he would not take away the first Covenant and begin something entirely new; rather, he *himself* would be that very Grace-Covenant. He now presents himself as a man, giving himself to mankind in both a divine and human way. Just as they had participated in him previously through his grace in the Covenant and the sacrifices in a spiritual way *in power* through a *medium*, they should now also participate in him in both a physical and spiritual way through the *medium* of bread and wine, and substantially participate in that same grace incorporated in the Covenant.

19. The reason was this: he wanted to give himself to them so they could participate in both his human and divine properties alike. He intended for them to participate in the grace (which had entered into humanity, destroyed Death, reopened human life, and brought it through Death) within a new human life. He did this so that the same New Life, arising from Christ's death and resurrection, might unlock their own lives which were imprisoned in death. Therefore, he gave them this new, unlocked human life from his own assumed human life, along with his flesh and blood, even under an *elementary medium* <note>A physical substance of the material world</note>, so that Faith might conceive it through a *medium*.

20. Therefore, there must be a *medium* so that, first, God's imagination <note>In this context, "imagination" refers to the divine creative will or the "imaging" of God's love into form</note> of his love, and secondly, the desire of man's faith, might come together and take hold of one another through a *medium*. For just as the participation in the Old Testament was done in a spiritual way, it should now be done in both a substantial <note>Physical or essential</note> and spiritual way *alike*. The Deity has now presented the Paschal Lamb in the humanity of Christ; and God's "imagination" (which formerly went into the animal sacrifice) now went into the humanity of Christ. Furthermore, the "imagination" of the humanity of Christ went with divine power into the *medium* of bread and wine, and through that medium—during the participation in the meal—it enters *into human life*.

It emphasizes that the sacrament allows believers to participate in Christ's resurrected life both spiritually and substantially.</summary>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>Chapter 3. The Third Chapter.</header>

## How the Disciples of Christ have Eaten and Drunk Christ’s flesh and blood: and how that is properly to be understood.

->I.<-

Reason should here move away from thinking in physical images toward that which is beyond images, and forsake the *folly* for which men strive. For this was not a participation in a physical image, though it is understood through one. Christ did not give his disciples his physical, creaturely, outward, and touchable fleshly humanity, as if it were a piece of his body. No, that is not logical, for *he sat by them at the table* and did not tear apart the physical substance of his body. Instead, he gave them his spiritual humanity—namely, the power and virtue of his body and blood, his own *Mumia* <note>original: "Mumia"; a term used in Paracelsian medicine and alchemy to refer to the vital life-force or "spiritual essence" inherent in a living body</note>, in which the Divine and human power is contained. This *Mumia* is a true human substance of flesh and blood, yet it is a spiritual flesh from which the visible image grows, and it is entirely one with that visible image.

2. Consider a comparison in the Sun, which *remains in its orbit* but presses outward with its luster, power, and whole substance—with everything that it is in essence, power, and substance—out of itself into the *whole world*. It gives itself to every element, substance, and creature; to all herbs and trees, and to every living creature. It works in all things that are willing to receive it, and yet it does not diminish itself. When it goes out from itself and *gives itself* to other creatures, it always remains whole and does not leave its own substance.

3. The Supper of Christ is to be understood in the same way. We see a comparison of it in the herbs and grass of the earth, which neither do, nor can do, anything *without* the power of the Sun. The plant sets the "jaws" of its essence wide open toward the Sun’s power and virtue; that is, it hungers only after that. Into that hunger, the Sun shines and kindles *the spirit*—namely, the sulfur <note>original: "Brimstone"</note>, salt, and oil of the herb. As soon as this is done, and the Sun’s virtue finds or perceives itself within the sulfur, salt, and oil of the herb, it *works within them*. From this, the herb attains a noticeable warmth; it conceives and takes the Sun’s virtue into itself, so that the Sun’s virtue and power become *substantial* within the herb. It grows with the herb and permeates <note>original: "tinctureth"</note> it, so that the herb becomes "solar" in nature and works toward the bringing forth of fruit.

<summary>The author explains that Christ's Last Supper involved a spiritual rather than a physical consumption of his body, using the Sun's influence on plants as an analogy for how divine substance permeates the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, spiritual humanity, Mumia, alchemy, solar analogy, Paracelsian philosophy, Jacob Boehme</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Last Supper, specifically focusing on the transition from the Old Testament sacrifices to the New Testament sacrament.</meta>

*...fruit of the vine, until that day that I will drink it new with you in my Father's Kingdom. Matthew 26, Mark 14, 1 Corinthians 11:23.*

18. This is now the true foundation, and it signifies, first, how the sweet grace of God's Covenant has given itself up into our humanity. Secondly, it shows how that same Grace-Covenant (which in the Old Testament gave itself to Israel as a blessing through sacrifices and the Passover Lamb <note>original: "Paschal-Lamb"</note>) now gives itself to humanity through the assumed human nature of Christ. This happens through a medium <note>original: "Medium"; an intermediate substance or means of communication</note>—namely, under the forms of bread and wine—to be eaten and drunk. Thirdly, it shows how he would not take away the first Covenant and begin something entirely new; rather, he *himself* would be that very Grace-Covenant. He now presents himself as a man, giving himself to humanity in both a divine and human way. Just as people had participated in him previously through his grace in the Covenant and the sacrifices in a spiritual way *in power* through a medium, they should now also participate in him in both a physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note> and spiritual way through the medium of bread and wine, and substantially participate in that same grace which is now embodied <note>original: "incorporated"</note> in the Covenant.

19. The reason for this was that he wanted to give himself to them so they could participate in both his human and divine natures alike. He intended for them to participate in the Grace—which had entered into humanity, conquered <note>original: "slaine"</note> Death, reopened the human life, and brought it through Death—within a new human life. He gave them this new, unlocked human life (derived from his own assumed human life, with his flesh and blood) even under an elementary medium <note>the physical elements of bread and wine</note>, so that Faith might grasp it through that medium. This was done so that the same New Life arising from Christ's Death and Resurrection might unlock their own lives, which were previously imprisoned in Death.

20. Therefore, there must be a medium so that, first, God’s "imagination" <note>In this context, "imagination" refers to God's creative will or the internal forming of his love</note> of his love and, second, the desire of human faith might come together and take hold of one another through that medium. Just as the participation in the Old Testament was done in a spiritual way, it should now be done in both a substantial and spiritual way *alike*. For the Deity has now presented the Passover Lamb within the humanity of Christ. God’s "imagination" (which formerly went into the animal sacrifices) now went into the humanity of Christ; and the "imagination" of Christ’s humanity went with divine power into the medium of bread and wine. Through the participation in those elements, that power enters *into human life*.

## Chapter 3: How the Disciples of Christ have Eaten and Drunk Christ’s flesh and blood: and how that is properly to be understood.

->I.<-

1. Reason should here depart from the physical image into that which is beyond images, and forsake the *folly* for which men strive. For it was not a participation in a physical image, but is understood through the medium of an image. Christ did not give his disciples his imaged, creaturely, outward, and palpable fleshy humanity, as if it were some piece of it. No, that is not consistent with the truth, for *he sat by them at the table* and did not tear the physical substance of his body. Instead, he gave them the spiritual humanity—namely, the power and virtue of his body and blood, his own *Mumia* <note>original: "Mumia"; a Paracelsian term referring to the vital, spiritual essence or "marrow" of a living being that contains its life-force</note>, in which the Divine and human power is understood. This *Mumia* is a true human substance of flesh and blood, yet it is a spiritual flesh from which the visible image grows, and it is entirely one with the visible image.

2. Consider a comparison in the Sun, which *remains in its orb*, yet presses forth out of itself into the *whole world* with its luster, power, and whole substance—with everything that it is in essence, power, and substance. It gives itself to every element, substance, and creature; to all herbs and trees, and also to every living creature. It works in all things, according to what each thing will receive. Yet, it does not diminish itself when it goes forth from itself and *gives itself* to other creatures; it always remains whole and does not depart from its own substance.

3. The Supper of Christ is to be understood in the same way. We see a comparison of it in the herbs and grass of the earth, which neither do, nor can do, anything *without* the power of the Sun. The plant sets the "jaws" of its essence wide open toward the Sun’s power and virtue; that is, it hungers only after that. Into that hunger, the Sun shines and kindles *the spirit*—namely, the sulfur <note>original: "Brimstone"</note>, salt, and oil of the herb. As soon as this is done, and the Sun’s virtue finds or perceives itself within the sulfur, salt, and oil of the herb, it then *works within it*. From this, the herb attains a perceptible warmth, and it conceives and impresses the Sun’s virtue into itself. Thus, the Sun’s virtue and power become *substantial* within the herb, growing with the herb and imparting its quality <note>original: "tinctureth"</note> to it. By this process, the herb becomes "solar" <note>possessing the qualities of the sun</note> and works toward the bringing forth of fruit.

<summary>The author explains that Christ's presence in the Eucharist is spiritual rather than physical, using the analogy of the Sun's rays nourishing a plant to describe how Christ's essence inhabits the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, spiritual flesh, Mumia, alchemy, solar analogy, nature of Christ, substance vs. image</keywords>

fruit of the *Vine, until that day when I will drink it new with you in my Father's Kingdom*, Matthew 26, Mark 14, 1 Corinthians 11:23.

18. This is now the true foundation, and it signifies, first, how the sweet grace of God's Covenant has given itself up into our humanity. Secondly, it shows how that same Grace-Covenant (which in the Old Testament gave itself to *Israel* as a blessing through sacrifices and the Paschal Lamb <note>The Passover lamb sacrificed by the Israelites</note>) now gives itself to man through the assumed humanity <note>The human nature taken on by Christ</note> under a *medium* <note>original: "Medium"; an intermediate substance or means</note>—namely, under bread and wine—to be eaten and drunk. Thirdly, it shows how he would not take away the first Covenant and begin something entirely new; rather, he himself would be that very Grace-Covenant. He now presents himself as a man and gives himself *into* man in both a divine and human way. Just as they had participated in him previously through his grace in the Covenant and the sacrifice in a spiritual way in power, they should now also participate in him in both a physical and spiritual way through the *medium* of bread and wine, and substantially participate in that same grace incorporated in the Covenant.

19. For the reason was this: he wanted to give himself to them so they could participate in both a human and a divine property alike. He intended for them to participate in the grace (which had entered into humanity, destroyed Death, reopened human life, and brought it through Death) within a new human life. He did this so that the same New Life, arising out of Christ’s Death and Resurrection, might unlock their own life which was imprisoned in death. Therefore, he gave them this new, unlocked human life from his own assumed human life, along with his flesh and blood, even under an *elementary medium* <note>A physical substance belonging to the material world</note>, so that Faith might conceive it through a *medium*.

20. Therefore, there must be a *medium* so that, first, God’s imagination <note>In this context, "imagination" refers to the divine creative will or the internal forming of a spiritual reality</note> of his love and, second, man’s desire of faith might come together and take hold of one another through a *medium*. For just as the participation in the Old Testament was done in a spiritual way, it should now be done in both a substantial and spiritual way alike. The Deity has now presented the Paschal Lamb in the humanity of Christ; and God’s imagination (which formerly went into the sacrifice) now went into the humanity of Christ. The imagination of the humanity of Christ went with divine power into the *medium* of bread and wine, and through that medium—during the participation in it—*into human life*.

### Chapter 3: How the Disciples of Christ have Eaten and Drunk Christ’s flesh and blood: and how that is properly to be understood.

-> I. <-

Reason should here depart from the physical image into that which is beyond images, and forsake the *folly* for which men strive. For it was not a physical participation, though it is understood through a physical one. Christ did not give his disciples his physical, creaturely, outward, palpable, fleshy humanity, as if giving a piece of it. No, that is not logical, for he sat by them at the table and did not tear the physical substance of his body. Instead, he gave them the spiritual humanity—namely, the power and virtue of his body and blood, his own <term>Mumia</term> <note>original: "Mumia"; in Paracelsian thought, this refers to the vital essence or spiritual "balsam" of a living body that carries its life-force</note>, in which the Divine and human power is understood. This <term>Mumia</term> is a true human substance of flesh and blood, yet it is a spiritual flesh from which the visible image grows, and it is wholly *one* with the visible image.

2. Consider a comparison in the Sun, which remains in its orbit but presses forth out of itself into the *whole world* with its luster, power, and whole substance—with everything that it is in essence, power, and substance. It gives itself to every element, substance, and creature—to all herbs and trees, and also to every living creature. It works in all things, whatever will receive it, and yet it does not tear itself apart when it goes forth from itself. Even as it gives itself to other creatures, it always remains whole and does not depart from its own substance.

3. The Supper of Christ is to be understood in the same way. We see a comparison of it in the herbs and grass of the earth, which neither do, nor can do, anything *without* the power of the Sun. The plant sets the "jaws" of its essence wide open toward the Sun’s power and virtue; that is, it hungers only after that. Into that hunger, the Sun shines and kindles *the Spirit*—namely, the sulfur, salt, and oil <note>In alchemy, these "Three Principles" (Sulphur, Salt, and Mercury/Oil) were believed to be the fundamental components of all matter</note> of the herb. As soon as this is done, and the Sun’s virtue finds or perceives itself within the sulfur, salt, and oil of the herb, it then *works* within it. From this, the herb attains a perceptible warmth; it conceives and impresses the Sun’s virtue into itself, so that the Sun’s virtue and power become *substantial* in the herb. It grows with the herb and tints it, whereby the herb becomes "solar" or sun-like, and works toward <insert>the bringing forth</insert> of fruit.

<summary>The author explains that the Eucharist involves partaking of Christ's spiritual "Mumia" rather than his physical flesh, using the Sun's influence on plants as an analogy for how divine substance inhabits earthly forms.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, Mumia, spiritual humanity, Sun analogy, alchemy, sulfur salt and oil, divine substance, Last Supper</keywords>

4. In the same way, we are to understand Christ’s Testaments. For He is the Sun of Life and the *Light of the world* <margin>John 8:12</margin>. According to His divinity, He is the Eternal Speaking Word—that is, the power and essence of the Godhead and the power of the Divine Light. According to His humanity, He is the formed, outspoken Word, which is entirely one with the Eternal Speaking Word. He has introverted our received humanity with the Eternal Word; he has turned the outward inward (the Substance of Time) and turned the inward outward (the Substance of Eternity). With the Divine Substance, through the human substance He received from us, He has become "pressing forth." Just as the sun presses forth from itself and gives of itself, so the Divine Substance gives itself forth through the human. <note>Note: The Divine Substance brings the human forth along with itself; for the Divine Desire moves toward the human essence, and the human essence moves toward the Divine "out-pressing" delight or desire.</note>

5. <note>Note: Because the Divine power and virtue excel the human, the human is brought forth through the Divine power. Thus, the human power and virtue give themselves together with the Divine, through the Divine power. The human power and virtue are a perception or discovery of the Divine, wherein the Divine finds itself in something—namely, in the formed substance of the Word of Power. In this, the Divine power loves itself as its own perceptible substance, just as the soul loves itself in its body.</note>

6. <note>Note: In this way, God has poured His love through the human substance in the humanity of Christ into Christ’s disciples. This happened in the same manner as the sun’s power in the *Spirit of the World* <note>original: *in Spiritu Mundi*</note>—as it mingles with the outflowing power of the stars and the *spiritual* sulfur, salt, and oil in the upper elements. The sun captures their power in its brilliance and gives itself to them, while their power gives itself into the sun’s power, and so they work together. By this, the light of Nature is unlocked. Through this working, they give themselves into the earth, herbs, trees, and all creatures, so that such a virtue exists within a plant or tree. From this, the growth or budding of creatures arises. In such growth or life, one must always understand two things: (1) an elementary, physical power, and (2) a super-elementary, solar, and astral <note>term: starry or celestial</note> power, where the solar and astral press forth with their strong power alongside the elementary.</note>

7. <note>Note: Now we see in herbs and all things that grow out of the—</note>

# Chapter 3: How Christ's Disciples fed on his flesh and blood.

...the Earth. When plants take hold of and eat the power and virtue of the sun and stars within them, they do not tear themselves apart or use a separate, physical mouth to do it. Instead, the essence of their desire—namely, the natural sulfur <note>original: "Brimstone"</note>, salt, and oil within the herb—acts as the *mouth*. This mouth takes hold of and eats the influence of the upper elements of the sun and stars into itself. In this same way, it is to be understood concerning Christ's disciples *and all other people: they have eaten and drunk Christ's flesh and blood* under the form of bread and wine, as if through a *medium*, using the essential mouth of a desiring faith. This is not done with a limited, physical grasp, but with the desire of faith.

8. **Note:** Yet, so that a person may rightly understand this, he must know: 
(1) The outward mouth, with its physical grasp, takes in the outward elementary substances of bread and wine. The mortal, temporary substance of man—that is, the *gross flesh* (which shall not and cannot inherit the Kingdom of Heaven, *John 6:63; 1 Corinthians 15:50*)—takes in and eats the bread and wine. For bread and wine are also earthly, temporary substances which go into the belly and are then expelled, like all natural food (*Matthew 15:17*). 

(2) The *true, original human*, who was created in *Adam*—who, before the animal-like coarseness of the fall, was a true human and an image of the visible world according to its inward spiritual foundation—that same human eats Christ's flesh. This is a spiritual, substantial flesh, eaten with the *essential desire*. 

**Note:** Understand that this is the flesh he took upon himself in Mary—namely, our *human* flesh—but not the outward foundation of the physical four elements. Rather, it is the inward holy element: the foundation from which the four elements proceed. It is not fire, air, water, and earth, but the eternal pure element, which is a substance of Paradise. This substance is hidden within the four elements. Understand it as the substance of eternity, from which time emerged into a <margin>visible</margin> substance. That same flesh is like a spiritual *mumia* <note>A term used in alchemy and Paracelsian medicine to describe the vital essence or "mummy" of a body that contains its life-force.</note> from which the visible body of man took its origin *by the word "Let it be"* <note>original: "in verbo Fiat"</note>. In *Adam*, this substance became blind to the Kingdom of Heaven when he turned his desire toward the coarseness of the four elements. This fleshly substance was *filled with heavenly living substance and made living again* when Christ became man. 

**Note:** That is what the true Adamic human eats, which remains hidden within the coarse shell of the four elements. And in that substance, the true human *eats Christ's passion, suffering, and death*. Through his victory, Christ's death has become an eternal life. This life, having risen out of the death of Christ, breaks our earthly evil will and has become a *death to our death*—that death which our outward nature mistakenly considers to be life.

9. **Note:** And here Christ's passion and dying are put on...

In this state, when the body dies here according to its four-elementary substance, it *rests*; and the four elements become dust and ashes. This same power and virtue of the true humanity rests in its principle until the coming again of the physical substance.

10. But (2) the soul, which is a spiritual fire, eats the supernatural, substantial love of the name JESUS in its desire, as if with the mouth of true faith. This Name has become a substantial power and virtue—namely, the supernatural wisdom of God—in which the *whole Deity* is understood to be working.

11. The soul’s mouth eats of this holy substance, by which the soul is turned into a burning fire of love. For the love of Christ gives *ens* <note>original: "Ens"; a philosophical term for "being" or "essence"</note> and substance to the soul’s fire. The fiery life of the soul takes this love-substance into its essence, and by this love-burning, the temple <margin>b Or, Sophia, the Wisdom of God.</margin> *Soph-JAH* <note>A combination of "Sophia" (Wisdom) and "Jah" (a name for God), representing Divine Wisdom.</note> is unlocked again. This is the substantial, holy, powerful light—the true Spirit which vanished in *Adam*. Just as a light comes out of a fire, another principle—the property of the *divine* power and virtue—goes out through the soul’s fire. In that outgoing power and virtue dwells the supernatural, non-physical God in Trinity. For this is the divine principle where God dwells, works, and wills in man, and in which the divine light is understood, which <margin>c Penetrates.</margin> *diffuses* itself through the true man, just as fire spreads through iron.

12. Note: Understand it correctly in this way: Man exists in three principles, or three beginnings. (1) The *visible man* is the visible world, which is both outward and inward—that is, both transitory and eternal. This consists of the pure element and the "efflux" <note>The flowing out or manifestation of spiritual substance into the physical.</note>, namely the four elements. (2) The *gross man*, with outward flesh and blood, is the body made of the four elements. However, the spiritual body in the *quintessence* (or fifth essence) is the holy element hidden within the four, which is neither hot nor cold, nor dry nor moist. But the *soulish ground* is not the element; it is grounded in the Eternal Word, where the fiery Word of the Father’s property breathed itself into the created body as another principle of an eternal nature.

13. Now, the third (3) *supernatural ground* of man is the true Spirit—meaning the substantial light and substantial wisdom. This came into man when the soul was breathed in, but it vanished when the soul turned itself away from the breathing Word and into its own will and into visible substance through its desire. Because the soul’s fire-life (derived from the Father’s property) could no longer reach that light, this "ground" vanished as far as the creature was concerned. It did not vanish in God, but only to the creaturely soul.

14. And when this holy ground of the soul and body was hidden, then the soul and body became a dark <margin>a dale or valley.</margin> dungeon, full of pain and torment. For God was hidden from it, and instantly the [spiritual] principles began to operate according to their own properties. First, the soul operated according to the property of God the Father’s anger, because the light that should shine in it was hidden. Second, the outward body began to operate within the four elements—namely, in heat and cold, and in all other properties of the stars' power and influence. Every awakened property impressed itself into a physical substance, which caused the body to become coarse, hard, and beast-like, and the true image of God became a *mask* <note>original: "Visard"</note> and a monster. This is the sin for which God’s power and virtue entered the human race again, took on humanity, and overflowed his divine power and virtue into us once more through a true and righteous humanity.

15. Since man exists in three principles—as an image of both the visible and invisible substance according to time and eternity—and since *Christ*, as true God and man, has also taken upon himself the visible and invisible substance in one undivided person, we must understand the *participation* in his substance through his testaments <note>The sacraments, specifically the Lord's Supper.</note> in the same way. Every principle in man eats and drinks of its own likeness again. Note: (1) The *outward, righteous* human image, made from the *dust* <note>original: "Limus," referring to the slime or clay of the earth from which Adam was formed.</note> of the earth, eats again of that same humanity of Christ which he received from man. (2) The soul eats of the divine love-fire. (3) The *heavenly divine substance* that vanished in *Adam* eats of the substantial wisdom. All of this is received, entirely unsevered, through the single mouth of faith. But note: (4) The *coarse beast* of the *coarse flesh*, which is merely a husk, receives only the outward husk of the sacrament—namely, the bread and wine. Under these, it receives the *judgment of God*, which breaks and kills the lust of this coarse body. Therefore, *man should distinguish the body and blood of the Lord* from the animal-like man who lacks faith. Such a person receives only judgment under the bread and wine; for as the mouth is, so is the food in the mouth.

16. Christ said in *John 6*: *"My flesh is the true food, and my blood is the true drink. Also: I am the bread that has come from heaven, which gives life to the world. He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood continues in me and I in him. But he who does not eat the flesh of the Son of Man has no life in him."* Then the Jews argued among themselves, saying, *"How can this man give us his flesh to eat?"* and they were offended by this saying. But he said: *"My words are spirit and life."* Note: This signifies that we should believe his words. He who understands and receives Christ's word into himself also receives that *Word* which has become flesh and blood; which Word has become...

# How Christ's Disciples fed on his flesh and blood. Chapter 3.

...become a true human substance, and that the mouth of faith comprehends it within itself—namely, the right hunger or desire of the true humanity, in a spiritual way and manner. **Note:** Just as the Word of divine power became man, so is it also embraced by faith, and is man in *his members also*.

17. That same spiritual, substantial Word takes on our humanity—meaning our spiritual humanity—and gives itself into it, working and dwelling there substantially in a spiritual manner. He dwells and works there just as *he dwelt* and worked in the body which he received from *Mary*. In that body, men saw our outward flesh and blood, yet within that visible form was the invisible substance of the spiritual world, of which he said in *John 3:13* that *he had come down from heaven.*

18. In this same way, that invisible spiritual substance of Christ unites and enters into our humanity through our faith's desire. It happens in the same manner as a spark of fire falls into *tinder* <note>original: "Tinder"; dry material used to start a fire</note> and kindles it, consuming the substance of the tinder and making it entirely fire. So also the substantial power and virtue of Christ consumes—within his "tinder," which is the human spiritual foundation—all evil influences in the mind, <margin>the senses</margin> thoughts, and will. Whether these evils came from the desires of the earthly flesh, from the Devil, or from the lusts of the world cast into the soul, that same spiritual fire consumes them all; for it <margin>takes possession of</margin> the life of man and rules it.

19. It is a light—specifically, a spiritual oil in the life of man—in which the true spiritual life (the life of the soul) burns. Without this spiritual oil, there is no true light or life in man, but only an astral light <note>original: "Astrall Light"; in Boehme's philosophy, the natural light of the stars and reason, which is inferior to divine light</note>. Without it, the true soul remains in a dark dungeon in its very essence, helping itself only with the astral light of the sun; for this reason, the soul also possesses vain earthly desires and lusts.

20. But the soul that attains this holy anointing oil of Christ within itself turns its longings and desires away from the vanity of the world and toward God's word and power. It becomes like a new young child lying in the mother's womb, allowing the mother to nourish it. Thus, the soul clings to Christ's essence, substance, and power, and eats from the trunk of the vine, Christ, *just as the branch eats from the trunk of the vine (John 15).*

21. Human reason should understand us correctly: it is not to be understood that Christ has entered into man with his gross, mortal flesh and blood. That physical flesh and blood is of no profit <note>referencing John 6:63, "the flesh profits nothing"</note> but is only like a receptacle or shell for the true spiritual man. Much less does the divine substance mingle itself with the physical bread and wine; so that when I behold the blessed bread and...

22. No: that cannot be. Bread and wine are but a *medium* <note>original: "Medium or meanes"</note> or means for it, just as the earthly mouth is a medium or means for the spirit. This tincture is above the elementary substance, where the four elements lie in the *temperature* <note>term: "Temperature" refers to a state of perfect balance or harmony between the elements, as they existed before the Fall</note>, which is a heavenly, paradisiacal power and virtue. That same tincture of bread and wine is the true *medium* through which Christ, the human tincture, gives himself into the human life. For *man does not live* <margin>f. By bread alone.</margin> *by the four elements alone* (Matthew 4:4). The gross food which enters the mouth does not sustain life by itself, but rather the inward power and virtue—namely, the *quintessence* <note>term: "Quintessence" or "fifth essence" is the purest, most concentrated essence of a substance, beyond the four physical elements</note>—in which the tincture lies like a spiritual fire.

Therefore, Christ pours or overflows his heavenly flesh and blood—namely, the holy anointing oil—into the life of man, through and with the nourishment of the true life: that is, through the tincture of bread and wine. This is not to say that the tincture of bread and wine is able to grasp such a thing on its own, but it is only a permissive *medium* for it. Just as the outward mouth of a man is only a *medium* through which the power and virtue of the tincture in the food is overflowed into the spiritual man; and because the highest tincture lies in bread and wine—which is nearest to the life of man and sustains it most of all—therefore Christ also ordained this testament <note>original: "Testament," referring here to the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper</note> under these elements.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how Christ's spiritual presence is received by the believer, moving from the specific act of the Eucharist to the broader nature of faith as a living substance within the soul.</meta>

# How Christ’s Disciples fed on his flesh and blood. Chapter 3.

Spiritual flesh and blood would have become merely bread and wine, and it would no longer continue to be as Christ said: *My words are Spirit and Life (John 6:63).*

26. Man's spiritual mouth takes in Christ's words and life through faith. These words are a power of his flesh and blood, where the divine Word has become a human substance. That same human and divine substantial Word is given to man along with the *tincture* <note>original: "Tincture"; here meaning the essential spiritual quality or "soul" of a substance</note> of bread and wine, acting as a *medium* <note>original: "Medium"; an intermediate means or instrument</note>, so that there may be a visible sign of what is being accomplished in the inward ground <note>the deepest, most fundamental part of the human soul</note>.

27. **Note, Note;** we should not depend on this means or *medium* alone, nor think that Christ's flesh and blood are only and exclusively participated in through the use of bread and wine; for human reason in this present time miserably errs in that belief. No, that is not so. When faith hungers after God's love and grace, it always eats and drinks of Christ's flesh and blood—both through the *medium* of the blessed food and without the *medium* of the food. Christ has not *bound* himself to bread and wine *alone*, but has bound himself to *faith*. He promised that he will be in man, he will continue in him, and man shall continue in Christ. His powerful, living Word continues substantially in faith. Faith may always eat of this substance (as it always could), for it is the nourishment of faith, in which faith exists and becomes a substance. **Note:** Through such participation, faith itself becomes a substance—namely, a spiritual flesh and blood of Christ, in which the living God in Trinity dwells, works, and wills.

28. This substantial faith in man is Christ himself, who remains in man and is the life and light of man. *That is the temple of the Holy Spirit, who dwells in us; as Paul says: "Do you not know that you are the temple of God, and that the Spirit of God dwells in you?" (1 Corinthians 3:16).* Also, *the temple of Christ is holy, which you are (1 Corinthians 3:17).* Also, *shall we who belong to Christ still be sinners? Far be it from us, for then Christ would be a servant of sin in us (Galatians 2:17).* This same substantial faith is also the branch on the vine of Christ; its power and virtue penetrate through the whole man (just as the sun penetrates a plant) and kill the lusts and works of the flesh.

29. The mortal flesh is not the substantial faith. That physical flesh does not comprehend <note>original: "comprehendeth"; in the sense of containing or grasping spiritually</note> Christ's flesh and blood, but rather experiences it just as a common stone experiences the "tincture" of gold. Just as fine gold lies and grows within the common stone, so also the love of Christ grows in the true life of man. It penetrates through that life as fire penetrates through iron. This does not mean such power resides in man's life naturally, or that man could receive the substance of God by his *own* power and virtue. No, it is given to him by grace, just as the sun of its own will gives itself to the plant. And the plant cannot, therefore, say, "I am the sun."

<summary>The author argues that while the Eucharist is a visible sign, Christ's spiritual presence is not limited to bread and wine but is received continually through faith. This faith becomes a "substantial" spiritual reality within the believer, transforming them into a temple of the Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, Faith, Indwelling of Christ, Grace, Spiritual Substance, Tincture, Temple of the Holy Spirit, Jacob Boehme</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mystical union between Christ and the believer, specifically focusing on the communal nature of the Lord's Supper.</meta>

Just as the Sun works within the world, yet the world is not the Sun, so also can a man *not say*: "I am Christ," simply because Christ dwells and works in him as in His express image. Rather, the creature is the passive vessel in which the Creator dwells and works.

<margin>Note.</margin>
30. We are members of Christ according to His humanity, and are therefore called *Christians* because Christ dwells and works in us. In our lives, we are His twigs and branches—those in whom He brings forth fruit through His power and virtue <note>original: "vertue"; here meaning inherent power or life-force</note>. O little children, it is a great mystery! Note this well: the use of this Testament <note>The New Testament or Covenant, specifically the Sacrament of the Altar</note>, where we especially partake of Christ’s flesh and blood under the forms of bread and wine, is ordained so that we should come together. We eat of one bread and drink of one cup, and therein receive Christ, so that we should *instruct* one another in what He has done for us. We are to declare His suffering, death, and bloodshed *to one another*, and *teach it to our children*. We should *bind* ourselves together in love through this act, and exhort one another that we are members of one body in Christ—that in Christ, we are all but one.

31. Just as the one and only Christ gives Himself to us *all* in common to be our one and only life, and loves us all in His one and only humanity—reaching out that same humanity with His great love and grace to us all in common under the bread and wine, and so binding Himself to us in one single participation—so should we also, in *such a coming together* and participation, *bind ourselves together in true love and faithfulness* as members of one body. We must seriously consider that in *such* participation, we are all but one in Christ. For we all partake of the one and only Christ, and in that same Christ, we are one single body, which is Christ living in His members. O little children, what a depth of mystery this is if we would rightly consider it! Satan, through the anger of God, has rent us asunder and made us *at odds* so that we have *opposite* thoughts. But here comes Christ with His love, making us all one single man again in Himself—which is He Himself living in us. Thus, all of us together have become branches rooted in His tree, which He Himself is; we all live from His virtue and substance and stand in one stock, which is Himself.

32. Therefore, we should rightly consider this and not draw near to this *Communion* with unworthy hearts and mouths, supposing it is enough that we partake of bread and wine. No, it is a brotherly, member-to-member bond and covenant. We bind ourselves through it to be one single man in Christ, and that same one man is every individual person in Christ. Therefore, our purpose in *such* a coming together should be that we, as members of one body, will bind ourselves fast through such participation and forsake Satan with his...

<summary>The author explains that the Lord's Supper is a communal "bond and covenant" that restores the unity of humanity by grafting believers as branches into the single "tree" of Christ's humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Communion, Mystical Body of Christ, Unity, Sacrament, Christian Fellowship, Branch and Vine, Covenant</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual nature of the Lord's Supper and begins a new chapter regarding the participation of the wicked in the sacrament.</meta>

his opposite will, and heartily love one another, *as Christ has loved us, and has given his life into death for us.*

33. To this end is this Testament of participation under bread and wine ordained: not to be understood as if it were a participation apart and separate, of which a Christian could not partake without the use of this ceremony. For if we are in Christ, and he himself is in us and is our life and light, and we are thus rooted in him in the substantial faith—which substance of faith he himself is—why then should that same life's desire not be able always to eat of it, if it only turns itself toward him? This ordinance is only a member-like bond of love, so that we might thereby instruct one another in what Christ has done for us, until he shall visibly come to us again in his assumed human image-likeness, and as our true High Priest be forever with us and in us.

### The Fourth Chapter.

## Of the difference of such participation; what the wicked receive by this Testament: and how a man should rightly prepare himself for it, so that he may be truly worthy.

->I.<-

As mentioned above, it does not lie in the conceit or supposition that one only needs to draw near with the body to such Communion and think: "When I have participated in the bread and wine with others, then I have received the true Testament of Christ, whereby my sins are forgiven me." No, *St. Paul* says: *He that distinguishes not the Body of the Lord from bread and wine, he receives it to his own judgment* <note>original: 1 Cor. 11:29. Modern translations often use "condemnation" or "judgment" for the Greek *krima*.</note>. Bread and wine cannot apprehend this Testament, much less the faithless, who come to it only out of custom and so that they may have the name of a Christian.

2. So also it does not stand in the priest's power, with his blessing, to bring the Body and Blood of Christ into the bread and wine; but it stands in the Divine Institution <note>The "Divine Institution" refers to the original act and command of Christ at the Last Supper, which gives the sacrament its power regardless of the priest's personal merit.</note>. This Institution is hidden from the wicked mouth, and it happens to such a person as it did to *Judas*, who, though he indeed also ate and drank of the bread and wine of the Supper;

<summary>This page argues that the Lord's Supper is a bond of love and a memorial of Christ's sacrifice, emphasizing that the sacrament's spiritual reality is accessed through faith rather than mere physical consumption or priestly ritual.</summary>
<keywords>Lord's Supper, Communion, faith, St. Paul, Judas, Divine Institution, High Priest, spiritual participation</keywords>

# Chapter 4. What the wicked receive in this Testament.

Supper; and although he was invited to the Testament, yet he did not receive Christ’s flesh and blood—that is, the Love of God. For after the Supper, *Satan entered into him* (John 13:27). This is as much as to say: the power and virtue of the Testament touched him, so that the mouth of his inward, false faith was stirred and opened. But as the mouth of his faith was, so was the Testament in his participation, as the Scripture says: *With the holy you are holy, and with the perverse you are perverse.* <margin>Psalm 18:26, 27.</margin>

3. He received the Testament of Christ, but it was the Judgment only. In the holy or the saints, this Judgment kills the earthly will of the Serpent <note>The "Serpent" refers to the sinful, fallen nature of humanity inherited from the Fall of Adam.</note>; that is, when the soul is capable of receiving the holy power and virtue, so that it has a "mouth of faith" which receives the Love in the Testament, then it also receives *along with it* Christ’s suffering, death, and resurrection, which kills sin in both soul and flesh. But the wicked mouth is not capable of Love; therefore, it receives only Christ’s suffering and death, *and not his resurrection*. For with his false opinion, he lays his desire—the mouth of his false faith—to Christ’s flesh and blood, and *kills Christ* within himself through this participation in the Testament. He is thereby guilty of the death of Christ, for with his false participation and false desire of faith, he touches the severe judgment of God found in Christ’s agony, wounds, and death.

4. Therefore, because he is only capable of receiving the Judgment, the Judgment of God—which killed Christ for the sake of our sins—is stirred in the soul and character of the wicked person during this Testament. This Judgment touches or stirs Christ’s wounds and death in his Testament through this participation of a false nature. For the false "seed of the serpent" <note>original: *Serpents-seede*; a reference to the spiritual offspring of evil or the devil.</note> stings Christ in the heel with this touch <margin>Or, pierces.</margin>. Christ offers the soul his Testament and desires to slay the Serpent; but while Satan holds the chief dominion in the soul, the soul will not receive it. Instead, through the essence of the soul, it casts its false, poisonous rays against <margin>Or, against.</margin> the wounds of Christ and desires to kill Christ.

5. Understand this: he desires to poison and infect that ground within man where Christ’s blood, death, and resurrection should be possessed. This evil stirs itself all the more in the false soul, just as it did in *Judas*. When he received this Testament, then evil was stirred in *Judas* and took hold of his life. Therefore the Scripture says: *After the piece of bread, Satan entered into him* (John 13:27). Because his false heart had stirred the judgment of God, that judgment also entered into him; it was already *within* him beforehand, but not manifested until he touched the Covenant in the Testament. So it went with him as it did with *Uzza*, *who unworthily touched the Ark of God* (2 Samuel 6:6, 7; 1 Chronicles 13:9, 10) <note>Uzza was struck dead for touching the Ark of the Covenant, illustrating the danger of approaching the holy without proper reverence.</note>. A wicked man ought to take note of this: that he should not touch this Testament without repentance for his sins; otherwise, he lays...

<summary>This page explains that when a wicked person like Judas partakes in the Lord's Supper, they receive God's judgment rather than His grace, effectively "killing" Christ within themselves. The author warns that approaching the sacrament without repentance is spiritually dangerous, comparing it to Uzza's fatal mistake in touching the Ark of the Covenant.</summary>

...lays his hand of false ownership upon Christ’s wounds, agony, and death, and will in the end receive the wages of Judas from it.

6. It is *not* to be understood as if the wicked person, who joins with others in the Covenant of the Testaments <note>original: "Testaments of Christ"; referring here to the Sacraments of Baptism and the Lord's Supper</note> of Christ, receives nothing at all. This is also to be understood regarding *Baptism*; for the Testament stands firm, and the Institution continues in power and virtue. The *faithless* person does not take away the Covenant or its power. The Covenant is present in such participation and use for everyone, but the participation is only as the "mouth" <note>The spiritual state or "vessel" of the receiver</note> is. God’s great love and grace—that is, the substantial love—does not give itself into the <margin>Or, anguish.</margin> wicked soul. Instead, the "Process of Christ"—including his pain, agony, scorn, and death—does indeed enter the wicked soul. In this, the "man-devil" (the wicked heart) pierces Christ in his wounds and makes itself guilty of the death of Christ.

7. The wicked person indeed participates in Christ’s descent into hell, in his anguish, and in his death; however, he is not capable of Christ's Resurrection, wherein Christ rules over death and hell. This is because his wicked will does not desire to die with Christ in Christ’s death—to die to his own wickedness, false dealings, and lifestyle—but desires only to rise and live with his sins in Christ’s Spirit. He wishes to rule in the Resurrection of Christ with his *earthly Lucifer* <note>The prideful, fallen nature of man</note>. Therefore, he treads upon the death of Christ with the feet of his false desires and does the same thing the *Pharisees* did to Christ. Therefore, it would be better for him not to boast of being a Christian, and better if he did not touch or meddle with Christ’s Testament.

### Of the Basis of Absolution.
### What Forgiveness of Sins Is.

->8.<-

<margin>d Or, ignorance.
e Or, sharp/precise.</margin>
The false conceit and gross ^d misunderstanding—when men teach that Christ’s Testaments destroy sin—requires a more ^e precise explanation. This is so that the wicked man may not hide himself under this idea, covering himself with Christ’s purple mantle and wearing it as a badge of falsehood. Christ’s bloodshed—specifically when he destroyed God’s anger with it and turned that anger into love—*is that* which destroys sin. In whoever worthily participates in Christ’s bloodshed, sin is destroyed through Christ's victory and resurrection by his blood and love. He who comes to it with a penitent heart for his past sins, and is angry at those sins, and has a strong purpose within himself *no more* to enter into them—he lays hold of the testamentary grace through faith.

9. It is false for one to delay his repentance until the participation in the Testament of Christ, and then to think that the same...

<keywords>Absolution, Sacraments, Penance, Resurrection, Judgment, Covenant, Blood of Christ, Hypocrisy</keywords>

will take away his sin; there is no forgiveness of sins, either by *Sacrament* <note>original: "Testament"</note> or *Absolution*, unless a man converts from sin and is *renewed* through earnest, sincere repentance and turning toward the grace of God in faith on Christ in the Holy Spirit: so that he takes up *another* will and resolution to depart from falsehood and wickedness.

10. For the forgiveness of sins—both within Christ’s Sacraments and *without* the use of them—is nothing else but this: when Christ arises from man’s death into man’s faith within the converting sinner; and when a new, obedient will arises out of his death and becomes a light in man’s life. So that the eternal night is turned into a clear day, then sin is *forgiven*. For if the eternal day of love dawns, then the night of the eternal darkness of God’s anger is turned into love, and there the *Wedding of the Lamb* is rightly celebrated. It is not celebrated with the unworthy heart, which runs to the Sacrament without repentance and without forsaking its sins, supposing that its sins are forgiven through the *laying on* of the priest’s hand and participation in the Sacraments.

11. The priest has *no* power to forgive sin; it does not reside in his own might and power. The power is in the *ordinance of Christ*. Christ within man—and also so far as He is in the priest himself—*forgives* sin to the repenting conscience. Absolution is but a *medium*, namely: an outward sign of this, so that we should receive one another in love and hearty forgiveness, and bind and reconcile ourselves in the love of the bonds of Christ, in his *Bride* <note>The "Bride" refers to the true spiritual Church.</note>, and in love receive one another into the *communion* of the body, namely: of the Bride of Christ.

<margin>The Church.</margin>
12. The *minister* of Christ takes the faithful, penitent man into the communion or congregation of Christ through an outward absolution in Christ’s stead. He is, with his absolution, a *medium* or means of that which Christ himself, through His absolution, does
<margin>Effectively/Workingly.</margin>
effectually in the inward ground of the soul, and so man is outwardly confirmed through such a *medium*. But if there is no faith and conversion there, but only a custom, then there is also no absolution; for the priest’s absolution without Christ’s co-working is powerless and dead. For the power does not reside barely in the outward ordinance and in the priest, but in the communion of the saints in Christ—namely, in the Bride of Christ. This communion takes the repenting man into their member-like fraternity; in the *faith* of that which is to come, the Spirit of Christ in His members receives him.

13. A wicked priest, in whom the Spirit of Christ is not present, *cannot* absolve him nor receive him, but the office of Christ through the word of His promise receives him. A false priest is but an outward, powerless instrument as to himself, and does no more with this than the...

the wicked priest with the *water-baptism*, who only pours the water and speaks the words without the co-working of the Spirit. But the Spirit of the Office looks not upon the unworthy minister of the office, but upon those who come to the office with faith. He <note>The Holy Spirit</note> absolves the believer through His office and receives him—along with the Bride of Christ <note>The Church</note> in whom He works—into the church or *congregation*. This is not done by a wicked *Pharisee* <note>original: "Pharisee"; here meaning a hypocritical or legalistic religious leader</note>, who himself is not capable of the office and only sits there as an *idol* which man worships. Such a man is himself but a devil full of falsehood and attributes to himself that which he *has not*.

14. There must be earnestness used in approaching such an office of the power of God, or else Christ is but mocked in it. Therefore, no man should rely solely upon the ordinance and think that the ceremony itself absolves him, or that he receives the testament merely for the sake of the ordinance and institution of Christ. If any man wishes to receive Christ into himself as a guest, he must then bring the temple of Christ—wherein Christ absolves him—with him into the ordinance. The absolution is but a receiving into the *communion* of the members of Christ; the priest receives him outwardly with the congregation, and Christ receives him in *faith* and binds Himself actively with him. This cannot be done without faith and an earnest turning toward God. And now, as Christ works inwardly in him, so also the congregation or *church* (namely, the Bride of Christ) works as fellow members in him through their prayers, and they all are but one in Christ.

<margin>Note.</margin>
15. But it is a snare of the Devil when the wicked think: "I am a sinner indeed, but I will eventually go to the office of Christ, to the communion of saints, *and will be absolved and receive Christ's testament so that my sins may be forgiven once; then I will sin anew once my old sin is done away*." This is what happens when many *begin to hang their heads a little and put on a show of piety*, and presently afterward return to their old customs, into all abominations and blasphemy. These people crucify Christ and <gloss>pierce him in his wounds</gloss>; it goes with such a person as it did with *Judas*, into whom Satan entered after he had eaten. It would be better if he stayed away from it altogether as long as he is not in earnest, if he thinks he is and remains a true Christian.

# Chapter 4. Of True Christianity.

[It is often wrongly assumed] that sin is satisfied for in such a way that a man needs only to give assent to the Gospel, and hold the history of what was done by Christ to be true; and that a man needs only and *barely* to go to his Testament, and there receive the grace which he left behind him, and comfort himself with it, and account it as a merit and *imputed grace* <note>original: "imputed Grace"; the theological concept that Christ's righteousness is credited to the believer's account</note>. It is not enough for a man to hear sermons preached, and to be baptized into Christ, and go to the [Lord's] Supper, so long as a man only keeps the custom. This by no means makes a Christian; it must be earnestness. No one is a Christian unless Christ *lives* and works in him, as Christ himself says: *Without me you can do nothing* (John 15:5). Also: *He that gathers not with me scatters* (Matthew 12:30).

17. A Christian must be one spirit in and with Christ, and will and work in the power of Christ. It is a *living, active*, working grace in a Christian; a continually burning fire, a *feeling power* and virtue. Although it is often covered with the lust of the flesh and the vanity of the world, yet it <margin>Glows.</margin> glimmers and burns in the heart like a fire, and reproves the flesh and the vain lust of untruth; it rejects the false way and does not desire it. That same *inward fire* is the *Spirit of Christ*, which without ceasing breaks the head of the serpent—namely, the lust of the flesh. The flesh has the will of this world, but that same kindled ground <note>The "ground" refers to the deepest essence or foundation of the soul.</note> has the *will of God*. If anyone is a Christian, then he will hate and be angry at the will of the flesh; he will be an enemy to his evil fleshly lust, and constantly complain and hold himself to be *unworthy*. He will continually, with the inward will of his soul, sink himself into the <margin>Note: Purest.</margin> purest grace, into God's mercy. He will *not say* of himself, "*I am a true Christian*," but will continually press with his desire into God's mercy and fly to grace, so that he might become a true Christian. In all his conduct, he will always account himself *too unworthy* of such grace, and in continual resigned humility, with weeping and prayer, only press into grace. His whole life will be a *continual repentance*, and he will evermore desire to take hold of grace, just as it has taken hold of him.

18. A true Christian is *affrighted* at sin when the lust of the flesh would work sin. Also, when he sees others work sin, then he *groans* within himself at it and wishes that such evil were not committed; it is an abomination in his eyes. He loves *truth* and *righteousness* and hates the false way. And although the earthly flesh often unawares, without any purpose of will, falls upon him with a sudden false lust and many times brings him to fall—as happened with David and Solomon and many saints, and is still done—yet such a man in whom the Spirit of Christ is does *not lie in sin*. Instead, the inward ground—namely, the incorporated grace in the Spirit of Christ—comes *quickly* with God's <margin>Strict.</margin> severe righteousness, in [divine] anger,

<summary>The author argues that true Christianity is not found in outward rituals or historical belief, but in a living, internal fire where Christ dwells and works within the soul. This internal grace leads the believer to constant repentance and a rejection of the sinful desires of the flesh.</summary>

<keywords>true Christianity, imputed grace, indwelling of Christ, inward fire, repentance, spiritual warfare, humility, grace vs. works</keywords>

anger, and enters into him in the conscience; just as the prophet *Nathan* entered into *David's* conscience, and touched his conscience, and awakened the fire of anger. Then *David* suddenly began with great sorrow to repent and acknowledge his sin, and entered into such earnest repentance that he would receive no comfort from all his friends. He would not allow his ears to be filled with comfort and the pleasant feelings of grace until he felt the grace of the Lord in his conscience. No flattery would set his heart at peace until the Lord *spoke into him with his grace* (2 Samuel 12:7).

### Of the worthy preparation to the holy Testament of Christ.

-> 19. <-

If anyone wishes to call himself a Christian, and comfort himself with the merits of Christ, and approach his Testament <note>The "Testament" refers here to the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper or Holy Communion</note>, and worthily receive it, let him consider his ways well. Let him look to his heart and see how it is formed: 

1. Whether he stands in such a desire as to be *willing* to die wholly to vanity; 
2. Whether it is his purpose to *go out* from all falsehood, unrighteousness, lying, and deceit, and whether he is resolved never to enter into them again; 
3. Whether he desires the grace of God in Christ with a clean, pure will; 
4. Also, whether he is sorry for his sin; 
5. Whether he finds and feels such a will in himself that from now on he will *wholly* depart from his former sins and blasphemies; 
6. And also whether he is so minded that he will yield up his *whole heart* and will to God's mercy; 
7. Also, whether he finds, feels, and knows a *place* in himself where he will lay up this high Testament—namely, the flesh and blood of Christ with his grace; 
8. Also, whether or not he has made *room* in his heart and whole soul for the Spirit of Christ, so that he may enter there as a living conqueror over death and hell, and may erect his kingly palace in his heart and soul; 
9. And whether he is *capable* of this, of which Christ says: *We will come to you and make our dwelling in you* (John 14:23); 
10. Also, whether the temple of the Holy Spirit in him is swept and purged with right repentance; 
11. Also, whether there is a right *mouth* <note>original: "Mouth"; referring to a spiritual capacity or "organ" of the soul to receive divine substance</note> in him that can rightly receive Christ's holy flesh; 
12. Also, whether the essence of his life is so formed that Christ, with his substance and with his love, may *continue* within it? For Christ says: *He that eats my flesh and drinks my blood continues in me and I in him* (John 6:56); 
13. Also, whether he finds in his mind that the stream of the living waters of *divine love* flows from him, so that he loves his God, and his brother and neighbor as himself; 
14. Also, whether he wishes and desires to *do good to his enemies*; 
15. Whether he accounts...

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual preparation required for receiving the Holy Sacrament, focusing on the renunciation of worldly ownership and the necessity of a humble, childlike heart.</meta>

16. Does he count anything in this world as *his own*, of which he says, "*This is mine alone*"? Or, in that which he has and possesses, does he account himself but a servant of God, and a steward to Him and his brother? Does he consider that he is but an *officer* and *servant* of God in his state and condition, and in his temporal goods—that none of it is his own, but God’s and his brother’s? 17. Also, does he trust God in his daily life, and keep and esteem his *life* as his Lord Christ did, who was but a *pilgrim* in this world and had nothing for His own, and also willingly gave up His *life* for His brothers? 18. Also, does he find a spark of such a will in himself?

20. Now, if he finds all this in himself, then he is rightly worthy and very fit for such a testamentary participation <note>original: "Testamentary participation"; referring to taking part in the New Testament or Covenant through the Lord's Supper</note>. *But if not*, and yet he finds such a *hunger* in himself that he would willingly be so and desires it, then he is *within the drawing of the Father toward the grace in Christ*. In that case, he should not long argue with his own reason or create doubts within himself. Instead, he should that very hour enter into such an earnest purpose that he will enter into sincere repentance and continually fly to the grace of God in Christ. He should pray that God will give him such a heart and will, and not attribute anything to himself as if he could attain it by his own power and virtue. Rather, he should simply and merely sink into the most pure grace, into the mercy of God. He should be in himself like a young child who only has a *desire* for the mother’s milk—one who cannot help himself but flies to the mother for her to help him.

21. His going to the Communion of Saints <note>The fellowship of believers, specifically here the act of taking Holy Communion</note> should be in *humility*, with a true reconciliation with all those whom he has wronged or who have wronged him. He should forgive all his enemies and wish the same for them which he desires for himself. With the fear of God and a penitent heart, in a right and earnest purpose, he may approach it. He must bring no selfish desire to the Testament of Christ, nor should he be willing to seize or understand such grace by his own ability. Instead, he should only sink himself down and wholly give himself up into that grace as one who is unworthy. He should cast himself upon grace, for it to do with him what it will. He must not *will* or desire the Spirit of *Divine Joy* as a personal possession <note>original: "propriety"; meaning something owned or controlled by the self</note>, but give himself up to it and sink down into grace, so that the Spirit of Grace may be in him *how* and *when* it wills.

anger, and enters into him in the conscience; just as the Prophet Nathan entered into David’s conscience, and touched his conscience, and awakened the fire of anger. Then David suddenly began with great sorrow to repent and acknowledge his sin, and entered into such earnest repentance that he would receive no comfort from all his friends. He would not allow his ears to be filled with comfort and the "tickling of grace" <note>The author uses "tickling of grace" to describe superficial or false spiritual comfort that does not address the root of sin.</note> until he felt the Lord's true grace in his conscience. No flattery would set his heart at peace until the Lord *spoke into him with His grace* (2 Samuel 12:7).

### Of the worthy preparation to the holy Testament of Christ.

### 19.

If anyone wishes to call himself a Christian, and comfort himself with the merits of Christ, and approach His Testament <note>original: "Testament"; here referring to the Holy Communion or the New Covenant established by Christ.</note>, and worthily receive the same, let him consider his ways well. Let him look to his heart and see how it is framed:

1. Whether he stands in such a desire as to be *willing to die* wholly to vanity;
2. Whether it is his purpose to *depart* from all falsehood, unrighteousness, lying, and deceit, and whether he is resolved never to enter into them again;
3. Whether he desires the grace of God in Christ with a clean, pure will;
4. Also, whether he is sorry for his sin;
5. Whether he finds and feels such a will in himself that from this point forward he will *wholly* go forth from his former sins and blasphemies;
6. And also whether he is so minded that he will yield up *his whole heart* and will to God’s mercy;
7. Also, whether he finds, feels, and knows *a place* within himself where he will lay up this high Testament—namely, the flesh and blood of Christ with His grace;
8. Also, whether or not he has made *room* in his heart and whole soul for the Spirit of Christ, so that He may *enter there* as a living conqueror over death and hell, and may erect His kingly palace in his heart and soul;
9. And whether he is *capable* of this, of which Christ says: *We will come unto you and make our dwelling in you* (John 14:23);
10. Also, whether the temple of the Holy Spirit in him has been swept and purged with right repentance;
11. Also, whether there is a right *mouth* <note>A spiritual "mouth" or capacity to receive divine substance.</note> in him that can rightly receive Christ’s holy flesh;
12. Also, whether the essence of his life is so framed that Christ, with His substance and His love, *may continue* therein? For Christ says: *He that eats my flesh and drinks my blood continues in me and I in him* (John 6:56);
13. Also, whether he finds in his mind that the stream of the living waters of divine love flows from him—that he loves his God, and his brother and neighbor as himself;
14. Also, whether he wishes and desires to do *good to his enemies*;
15. Whether he accounts—

<summary>This page outlines the rigorous self-examination required for a Christian to worthily receive the Sacrament, emphasizing a total turning away from sin and the creation of a spiritual "dwelling place" for Christ within the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Repentance, King David, Nathan the Prophet, Holy Testament, Communion, Self-examination, Indwelling of Christ, Divine Love</keywords>
