# Chapter 4. Of the worthy preparation to the Holy Testament.

15. <note>The numbering continues from the previous page.</note> Does he value anything in this world as *his own*, of which he says, "This is mine alone"? 16. Or, in that which he has and possesses, does he consider himself merely a servant of God, and a steward to Him and to his brother? Does he consider that he is only an officer and servant of God in his status and condition, and in his temporal goods—that none of it is his own, but belongs to God and his brothers? 17. Also, does he trust God in his daily life, and keep and value his *life* as his Lord Christ did, who was but a *pilgrim* in this world and had nothing for his own, and also willingly gave up his *life* for his brothers? 18. Also, does he find even a spark of such a will within himself?

20. Now, if he finds all this in himself, then he is truly worthy and very fit for such a participation in the Testament <note>original: *Testamentary participation*; referring to the Lord's Supper</note>. *But if not*, and yet he finds such a *hunger* in himself that he would willingly be so and desires it, then he is *being drawn by the Father* toward the grace in Christ. In that case, he should not argue long with his own reason or create doubts within himself. Instead, he should that very hour enter into such an earnest purpose that he will begin sincere repentance and continually fly to the grace of God in Christ. He should pray that God will give him such a *heart* and will. He must not credit himself, as if he could attain it by his own power and virtue, but should simply and purely sink into the most pure grace—into the mercy of God. He should be in himself like a young child who only has a *desire* for the mother's milk; who cannot help himself, but flies to the mother so that she may help him.

21. His approach to the Communion of Saints should be in *humility*, having achieved a true reconciliation with all those whom he has wronged, or who have wronged him. He should forgive all his enemies and wish the same for them as he desires for himself. With the fear of God and a penitent heart, and with a truly earnest purpose, he may approach the sacrament. He should bring no selfish desire to the Testament of Christ, nor should he try to seize or understand such grace by his own ability. Instead, he should only sink himself down and wholly give himself up into that grace as one who is unworthy. He must cast himself upon grace, for it to do with him what it will. He should not at all seek to demand the spirit of *divine joy* as if it were his own property <note>original: *propriety*; meaning something he has a legal right to possess</note>, but should give himself up to it and sink down into grace, so that the Spirit of Grace may be in him *how* and *when* it wills.

*I come to you upon your call, since you have invited us poor men to come and you will refresh us,*
<margin>Matthew 11:28</margin>
*let it be done to me according to what you say. However you wish, I give myself up herewith wholly and altogether; do with me, a poor unworthy man, according to your grace, as you will. I will eternally be your own. Break my will now, and govern it with your will; I can do nothing and am able to do nothing but sink now, wholly and altogether, into your grace.*

23. Such a person who wholly gives himself up to God, and continues standing in such resignation <note>original: "resignation"; here meaning the act of surrendering one's own will to God</note> with his *will*, will in the end—when grace moves within him—find and feel what grace and divine love are. When the divine fire is kindled in his *life*, then he will feel and taste what Christ in him is, and quickly find how he has become another man with different thoughts and a different will.

24. And then he is a true Christian: when Christ’s love-fire strives against God’s anger in the conscience. It will be set before that man, through Christ’s own journey <note>original: "processe"; referring to the pattern of Christ's life and trials</note> in this world, that he must follow after *Christ* in his suffering, anguish, pain, scorn, and persecution. He must take the cross of Christ upon himself and be made conformable to *Christ’s image*. In this state, there is inward strife against sin and the fleshly lusts that are in him, so that he despises himself and hates evil desires. Outwardly, he faces contempt, scorn, trouble, and misery; the world accounts him a stranger and a fool. His own reason looks upon itself as foolish, and he himself—regarding the matters and social life of this world—will be a fool and hate everything which his flesh loves. In this state, there is no one to flatter him; instead, all his "good friends" shun him and withdraw themselves, except only a *few* of the children of God who take notice of his condition and whom God sends to him for comfort. Then he may think that he, at that time, hangs upon the cross with Christ. He behaves himself in such a way that he would willingly even die with Christ for the sake of professing the truth, in the hope that he shall also rise with Christ in his victory and live eternally in Christ.

25. This is a true Christian and one worthy of the *Communion of Saints*: one who has entered into this process and lives within it. All others—who go only out of custom and consider Christ’s Testament to be merely an outwardly imputed *grace* <note>original: "imputed Grace"; the theological concept of Christ's righteousness being credited to a believer's account without an internal change</note>, and who appropriate it to themselves as a work done through belief, receiving it as a gift but refusing to be *born again* or to become another man of different thoughts and will—and keep...

the defiled cloak of sin in their conscience, and quickly return to their old ways: all these people are unworthy, unfit, and incapable of receiving the Testament. They receive it *only* unto judgment, as was mentioned before.

### *The Fifth Chapter*

## Concerning the Contention, Disputation, and Strife of the Learned about Christ’s Testaments: What they achieve by it, and what should be believed regarding it.

-> 1. <-

I will add this chapter not out of a desire to interfere with anyone’s good conscience, but for the comfort of the *simple* children of Christ. These people are led into error by men who shut them up in rigid opinions and seduce them away from true understanding through contention. They turn Christ’s Testaments into a mere *den of murder* <note>original: "Den of Murther"; a reference to Matthew 21:13, suggesting that violent theological disputes kill the spirit of the sacrament</note> and bind the conscience in snares and chains. To these simple children, I will explain what they should think of the contentious disputations of those *learned* in human reason, and what those men actually establish by their strife.

2. Christ’s Testaments are nothing else but a bond and covenant between God and man—a member-like union <note>term: "Memberlike union"; the idea that believers are individual limbs or parts of the single body of Christ</note> of the children of men. In this union, God has bound himself to man again after the Fall through the humanity of Christ, to be their loving God. Now, all those who—*outside* of this member-like bond and covenant of love—contend and dispute about concepts, opinions, and knowledge, are merely following their own selfish nature without Christ. There is no true understanding in any such person, *for no one knows the Son except the Father, and no one knows the Father except the Son and those to whom the Son chooses to reveal him* <note>original: "none knoweth Christ, but the Father onely, and those to whom the Father will reveale him"; citing Matthew 11:27</note>.

3. Now, if Christ is revealed to and *within* a person, then that person has no contentious disputation or strife with anyone regarding that knowledge and skill. Instead, he is humble and considers himself unworthy of such knowledge. He despises no one because of a *difference* in their spiritual gifts, but is in love with all people. He lets every man have his own opinion and gives to his neighbor only his "love-will" <note>term: "Love-will"; a heart motivated entirely by divine love and the desire for the neighbor's well-being</note>. His only concern is how he may be a *member* of Christ and of his brothers and sisters.

<summary>The author warns that those who return to sin after the sacrament receive judgment rather than grace, and begins a critique of learned scholars who replace the "covenant of love" with divisive theological arguments.</summary>
<keywords>Testament, Covenant, theological strife, humility, member-like union, love-will, Matthew 11:27</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of religious sectarianism and the disputes over the nature of the Eucharist (the Lord's Supper).</meta>

4. But because men *run into so many opinions*, and strive, contend, and dispute about them—despising one another for the sake of the *letter* <note>The literal or outward wording of scripture as opposed to its spiritual meaning.</note>, and scorning and condemning each other to the Devil—the simple Christian should know that in all such strife there is *no true understanding*. It is mere pride and *Antichristian* <margin>a. Matters.</margin> ^a actions, or a miserable confounding of the words of Christ. Nothing arises from it but disunion, insecurity, and enmity; nothing else is achieved by it but the Devil's will.

5. And this happens because they present themselves as *Teachers* of the Kingdom of Christ, and yet *are not sent* or known by God. They take all their material from others, exchanging words and letters. They contend and dispute about the "art" of how men may skillfully set letters together to form opinions—debating which composition is most clever—and then they force *consciences* into that art. But they themselves *do not understand* what they are doing. Their knowledge is stuck purely in the *letters*, and they do not have the *Living Word*, Christ, in them to give testimony to the literal word. Had they Christ and his love within them, they would share that same love and show Christ to others through themselves. They would walk in such a way that *people* could see they were Christ’s children. But while they only contend and dispute, and refuse to love and live that way themselves, their claims are false and nothing but a hypocritical show.

6. To see that this is true, let men look at the theories about which they contend. One group says, *Christ is substantially present under the bread and wine*. They also say, *the bread and wine are changed into the Testament of Christ, and there is a transubstantiation of the bread and wine*. Thus, they want to receive Christ into the bestial and mortal man—yet that "beast" <note>The physical, unregenerate human nature.</note> gains nothing <margin>b. Text: In holiness.</margin> ^b in terms of holiness. The second party says, *It is only a sign and symbol of the body of Christ, showing that he was broken or died for us*. They deny the <margin>c. All-present, presential.</margin> ^c present, substantial participation in Christ. They understand nothing concerning man—what and how he is, and what he lacks and needs so that he may attain God's salvation again. The third party tries to handle the matter best by keeping to the words of the Institution <note>The "Words of Institution" are the words spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper.</note>, saying, *Christ is participated with and under bread and wine; that is, Christ’s flesh and blood are eaten and drunk under bread and wine*. But they still have no understanding of how that happens, or what part of man is capable of receiving such a gift. They refuse to know anything of the inward ground and the true Adamic humanity <note>The original human nature as created in Adam, which must be restored.</note>. They also deny the substantial inhabiting of Christ within the believer and are as far from understanding as either of the other two. Neither will they learn how this participation is worked, but instead depend purely on the dead letter of the text while they lash out and thunder with insults, scorn, accusations of heresy, and blasphemy.

<summary>The author argues that sectarian disputes over the Eucharist stem from pride rather than spiritual understanding, critiquing three common theological positions for focusing on the "dead letter" rather than the living presence of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, sectarianism, religious dispute, transubstantiation, spiritual vs. literal, Adamic humanity, hypocrisy, theology of the sacraments</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
7. Every party strives only for a literal image, and wants to have Christ bound to their <margin>imaginary</margin> image-like opinion. They want to be the patron of that same image, and they do this for no other reason than to attain glory, honor, and high esteem by it. This is evident because they all disgrace and despise one another, just so each can maintain his own image-like opinion and be called and esteemed as <margin>or, masters and dispensers</margin> lords of the mysteries of Christ. Yet, in the depths of their hearts, they respect nothing but the earthly Lucifer <note>original: "Earthly Lucifer"; referring to pride and the fallen state of self-will</note>, seeking temporary honor and self-will; they want to be <margin>venerated</margin> worshipped as Christ. Every party wants it so that men should worship their image—that is, their *framed* literal opinion.

8. They use Christ’s Testaments <note>original: "Christs Testaments"; referring here to the Sacraments, specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper</note> to distinguish their images and create a *sect* with them. They cry out, "Here is the Church of Christ; there is heresy and seduction! Cleave unto me, for here is Christ!" With this, they embitter the hearts of kings and princes, as well as the minds of the *people*, so that one brother—for the sake of an image-like opinion—despises, disgraces, slanders, and blasphemes another. He accounts him a devil and cries out against him as such, and will even start wars, bloodshed, and the desolation of land and people for the sake of such *idol-images*. All of these are a mere empty shell without fruit, and they belong to the fiery world <note>original: "fiery World"; the realm of judgment or spiritual purification</note> for separation.

9. Men have brought things to such a pass that the people *think* they are saved if they simply cleave to and allow the opinion of such leaders, whether they understand it or not. They think they are safe as long as they honor a *sectarian* name and hold the patron of their opinion to be right. When someone hears that leader's name, he cries, "Yes, yes, that is right!" and yet he does not know what it actually is. Men have so completely blinded and seduced the simple-minded that people look only at men's *names* and think Christ is found within a specific opinion. What is even worse, they compel people by force into such image-like opinions. They have so blinded men that they will even forsake their bodies and goods for the sake of an opinion which they do not fundamentally understand, and they will persecute, hate, and kill one another for it.

10. Every one of them cries out that people want to *take away* the true doctrine—that is, the true faith—*from them*. Yet they do not actually possess it, nor is it found in their opinion. Thus, the world is entirely filled and blinded with opinions. Men suppose that if a person uses Christ’s Testaments according to the opinion of his patron, then there is blessedness and salvation in it, and otherwise there can be no salvation. They believe that whoever does not form their thoughts according to that same opinion, or does not hang and cleave to them, cannot be saved; they also claim such a person is no true member of right Christendom.

<summary>The author critiques the religious factions of his day for turning the Sacraments into sectarian tools used to seek personal power and social status. He argues that people have been blinded into following names and opinions rather than the true spirit of Christ, leading to senseless persecution and war.</summary>
<keywords>sectarianism, religious conflict, Sacraments, spiritual blindness, idolatry, church authority, false piety</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against sectarian conflict, moving from the critique of religious disputes to the necessity of an internal, living faith in Christ.</meta>

# Of the Contention of the Learned: Chapter 5

11. Men do so miserably contend about such image-like opinions <note>original: "Image-like opinions"; refers to mental constructs or dogmas that people worship instead of the living God</note> *that the churches are made into nothing else but mere houses of contentious disputation and spiritual dens of murder.* This murder ultimately leads to wars and bloodshed, and mere disgrace and dishonor is done to Christ for the sake of his holy gifts and testaments. In truth, nothing else is achieved by this except *as Israel danced about the Golden Calf and made an idol-calf to be their God, rejoicing in their own handiwork—namely, in their idol-image—and lost their God. Therefore, the anger of God was also kindled against them and devoured them all in the wilderness, so that such servants of idols and images could not enter into the Promised Land.*

12. Loving brethren, do not contend about opinions; all contentious disputation is the image of an idol. Concerning his omnipresence, Christ told us of no opinion when he said, *"He will be with us every day to the end of the world"* (Matthew 28:20). He does not say he is in *this or that opinion*, but where he signifies his presence, he says this: *"As the lightning goes forth and shines even to its setting, so shall also the ever-enduring coming of the Son of Man be"* (Matthew 24:27). And he says, *"The kingdom of God is inwardly within you"* (Luke 17:21).

13. Christ’s light and power rise up in his children in the inward ground <note>original: "inward Ground"; the deepest part of the human soul or essence</note> and shine through them throughout the *whole* course of their life. In that wellspring of light is the kingdom of God in man. Now, if a person does not have this, let him contend and dispute as much as he wants; he will never bring it into himself through any opinion. But if he has it, even streams of *love* flow out of that same wellspring. It needs no opinion. Let him only see to it that Christ’s kingdom is born in him—that Christ becomes man in him. Otherwise, he is no Christian, regardless of his opinion. He must stand as *a branch in the vine of Christ* (John 15). Opinion does not help him, but true faith does—which is active through the breaking forth of love and performs good works (Galatians 5:6). If he does not have the works of love, then he also has no faith. Opinion does not create salvation; rather, it is Babel <note>original: "Babell"; referring to the Tower of Babel as a symbol of the confusion of languages and religious division</note>, a confusion of the tongues of the one and only Love. No one is a Christian unless he loves his neighbor and desires to do him good.

<margin>Or, childlike.</margin>
14. Outwardly imputed grace <note>original: "imputed Grace"; the theological concept that Christ's righteousness is credited to a believer's account</note> without innate <term>filial grace</term> <note>original: "filial Grace"; the grace of being a true child of God, characterized by an internal transformation rather than just a legal status</note> is entirely false. When Christ works in a man, then he is a Christian, and then the sufferings, merits, and satisfaction of Christ benefit him. When he has Christ within himself as an advocate—the one who has already accomplished the work—so that Christ may also do it within him and draw his merits to him, then the kingdom of God is in that *merit*. Otherwise, all image-like opinions are false. No work pleases God except what he himself works in man through *his Spirit*. Therefore, let us be the children of Christ, and not the children of images.

<summary>The author argues that religious disputation is a form of idolatry and that true Christianity is found in the internal presence of Christ and the practice of love, rather than in dogmatic opinions or external "imputed" grace.</summary>
<keywords>religious conflict, idolatry, internal kingdom, love vs. opinion, filial grace, indwelling of Christ, Babel</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page concludes the second book regarding the Holy Supper, focusing on the spiritual requirements for receiving Christ's kingdom and the necessity of humility over intellectual pride.</meta>

15. Christ has imparted his kingdom to us in his Testaments. He who wishes to receive it must be his child; otherwise, there is no inheritance. To practice repentance is better than to desire to know much. If knowledge is not given by God to a man, then he will not be able to understand the basis of the divine, secret mystery. But if it is given to him, he needs no image <note>original: "Image"; likely referring to external idols or mental constructs used to represent God</note>. To continue in the humility and simplicity of Christ, and to cling to his *love* and **grace** without despising anyone, is good Christianity. All that speaks of itself <note>meaning: speech originating from human ego rather than divine inspiration</note> is *Babel* <note>original: "Babell"; a reference to the Tower of Babel, symbolizing confusion and prideful human systems</note>; in *Christ*, we are all members, and all one. *A M E N.*

->(⸪)<-
